coupling introduced \emph{only} by the shared neural network
(\Cref{chap:equiformer}) that predicts both components jointly from the
same input graph, exactly as \Cref{sec:sigmadock-state-space} established
for SigmaDock and as \Cref{chap:sigmaflow} now confirms, component by
component and directly against the code, holds equally for SigmaFlow.

\clearpage

% ================================================================
\part{SigmaFlow}
\label{part:sigmaflow}
% ================================================================

\chapter{SigmaFlow: Full Reconstruction}
\label{chap:sigmaflow}

We now perform, for SigmaFlow, exactly what \Cref{chap:sigmadock} did for
SigmaDock: assemble the already-derived theory
(\Cref{chap:flow-matching,chap:riemannian-fm}) with the specific network of
\Cref{chap:equiformer} into a complete, code-verified reconstruction.
Because \Cref{chap:sigmadock} already derived the state space
(\Cref{sec:sigmadock-state-space}), the fragmentation and triangulation
machinery (\Cref{sec:sigmadock-fr3d,sec:triangulation-precise}), the
symmetry argument (\Cref{sec:sigmadock-symmetry-precise}), the input graph
and backbone (\Cref{sec:sigmadock-graph}), and the fragment-reconstruction
map $\varphi$ (\Cref{sec:fragment-reconstruction}) --- all of which
\Cref{tab:sigmadock-vs-sigmaflow-precise} below confirms are unchanged ---
this chapter focuses entirely on what \emph{does} change: the probability
path, the training target, the loss, and the sampler.

\section{What changes, and what does not}
\label{sec:sigmaflow-overview}

trees. Rows above the double rule are unchanged; rows below are what this

\label{tab:sigmadock-vs-sigmaflow-precise}\\
\toprule
Component & SigmaDock (\Cref{chap:sigmadock}) & SigmaFlow \\
\midrule
\endfirsthead
\toprule
Component & SigmaDock & SigmaFlow \\
\midrule
\endhead

\section{A time-convention warning, confirmed at the source level}
\label{sec:sigmaflow-time-convention}

\Cref{rem:time-convention-global} fixed a global convention for this
document ($t=0$ noise, $t=1$ data) and warned that SigmaDock's own native
convention runs the opposite way internally
(\Cref{rem:time-convention-scratch} in \Cref{chap:sigmadock}, and
\Cref{sec:sigmadock-sampling}'s observation that the diffusion sampler's
own timestep array decreases from $t_{\max}=1$ to $t_{\min}$). We record
here, as a fact independently confirmed against \emph{both} source trees
during the preparation of this document (not merely inferred from
exposition), that this is not a convention chosen for pedagogical
convenience in this monograph but a genuine, load-bearing difference
between the two codebases' own internal time variables:

\begin{itemize}
\item \pyfunc{R3Diffuser}/\pyfunc{SO3Diffuser} (SigmaDock): $t=0$ is clean
data ($\alpha_{t=0}=1$, no noise); $t=1$ is the fully-noised end
(\pyfunc{sample\_ref} literally evaluates the forward kernel at $t=1$).
\item \pyfunc{R3\_FlowMatcher}/\pyfunc{SO3\_FlowMatcher} (SigmaFlow): $t=0$
is the noise/source end ($X_0\sim p_{\mathrm{init}}$ drawn internally by
\pyfunc{conditional\_probability\_path}); $t=1$ is the data end
($X_1=z$, the training target).
\end{itemize}

This document's global convention (\Cref{rem:time-convention-global})
matches SigmaFlow's own native convention exactly, and every formula
attributed to SigmaDock throughout \Cref{chap:sigmadock} was, correspondingly,
already relabelled via $s=1-t$ before being stated (\Cref{rem:time-convention-scratch}
there). Any reader cross-referencing a formula directly against SigmaDock's
own source should apply this relabelling explicitly; failing to do so is,
per this document's own code-research process, a genuine and easy source of
sign errors, exactly as this document's introduction anticipated.

\section{Translation flow matching, exactly as implemented}
\label{sec:sigmaflow-translation}

\begin{codebox}
\textbf{Mathematical object:} the source distribution, conditional path,
and both forms of the conditional vector field of
\Cref{prop:linear-path-scratch} \\
\textbf{Implementation:} \file{r3\_flow\_matcher.py}, class
\pyfunc{R3\_FlowMatcher} (56 lines total) \\
\textbf{Interpretation:}
\pyfunc{sample\_init}$(n)=\texttt{randn(n,3)}$ ($X_0\sim\mathcal N(0,I_3)$
per fragment, no rescaling by any $\sigma_{\min}$-type parameter);
\pyfunc{conditional\_probability\_path}$(x_1,t)$ draws $x_0$
\emph{internally} (unlike the diffusion side's
\pyfunc{forward\_marginal}, which takes $x_0$ as an argument --- a
structural difference reflecting that flow matching's source is drawn once
per training example and never needs to be revisited, whereas diffusion's
$x_0$ is the fixed data point being noised), computes
$x_t=(1-t)x_0+tx_1$ (\Cref{prop:linear-path-scratch}'s interpolant) and
returns $u_t=x_1-x_0$, the \emph{constant-in-$t$, endpoint} form --- this
is the quantity actually regressed against during training
(\Cref{sec:sigmaflow-loss}). \pyfunc{calc\_trans\_vector\_field}$(x_t,x_1,t)=(x_1-x_t)/(1-t)$
implements the \emph{current-point} form
\eqref{eq:linear-path-vf-scratch}, singular as $t\to1$ by construction
(the docstring itself notes $t$ must lie in $(0,1)$); this form is
\textbf{not} used during training (confirmed by tracing every call site in
\file{sigma\_flow\_generator.py}), only at sampling time
(\Cref{sec:sigmaflow-sampling}) and for the \texttt{use\_true\_vector\_field}
oracle debugging path, where $x_0$ is not naturally available but the
current point $(x_t,t)$ is. \pyfunc{euler\_step}$(x_t,v_t,dt)=x_t+v_t\,dt$,
the plain (non-geodesic, since $\RR^3$ is flat) Euler step of
\Cref{sec:cfm-sampling-scratch}, and the module's own inline comment notes
this step is \emph{exact} (zero discretisation error, for any step size)
specifically because $u_t$ is constant along the true conditional path ---
a special property of the linear path, not shared by the rotational
component's geodesic (\Cref{sec:sigmaflow-rotation}) once the network's
prediction, rather than the true field, is substituted in. The constructor
parameter \texttt{sigma\_min} is accepted but \textbf{provably unused}: it
is stored (\texttt{self.sigma\_min = sigma\_min}) and never read anywhere
else in the file, with an explicit docstring noting it is ``reserved for
future noised OT path, currently unused --- the linear path below is
deterministic''.
\end{codebox}

\section{Rotation flow matching, exactly as implemented}
\label{sec:sigmaflow-rotation}

\begin{codebox}
\textbf{Mathematical object:} the source distribution, conditional path,
and both forms of the conditional vector field of
\Cref{sec:fm-so3-scratch} \\
\textbf{Implementation:} \file{so3\_flow\_matcher.py}, class
\pyfunc{SO3\_FlowMatcher} (63 lines total) \\
\textbf{Interpretation:}
\pyfunc{sample\_init}$(n)=$\pyfunc{so3\_utils.sample\_uniform}$(n)$, the
exact Haar sampler of \Cref{prop:haar-so3}'s code correspondence
(\Cref{rem:so3-fm-prior}'s claim, confirmed directly). The class's own
module-level comment states the convention adopted is
``right-trivialisation''. That \emph{word} does not match this document's
attribution (\Cref{rem:trivialisation-terminology}: what the code calls
right-trivialisation is what we call left-trivialisation), but the
\emph{object} does, and the object is what matters: direct inspection of
\pyfunc{euler\_step}$(R_t,v_t,dt)=R_t\,\exp(v_t\,dt)$ shows it
right-multiplies the exponential onto $R_t$, which is exactly
\Cref{prop:euler-body-triv}'s \textbf{body-frame} update. The velocity the
network supplies is therefore $\omega^{\mathrm{bd}}$, however either side
chooses to label it.
\pyfunc{conditional\_probability\_path}$(R_1,t)$ draws $R_0$ internally,
computes $\Delta=R_0^\top R_1$, $\Xi=\log(\Delta)\in\so(3)$
(\Cref{def:so3-interp-detail}'s notation), sets $u_t=\Xi$ (a $[n,3,3]$
skew-symmetric-matrix-valued, i.e.\ $\so(3)$-valued, not yet
vee-mapped-to-$\RR^3$ quantity --- the \emph{constant-in-$t$} form of
\Cref{prop:so3-constant-velocity}, and the training target,
\Cref{sec:sigmaflow-loss}), and $R_t=R_0\exp(t\Xi)$
(\Cref{eq:so3-interp-detail}, confirmed exactly).
\pyfunc{calc\_rot\_vector\_field}$(R_t,R_1,t)=\log(R_t^\top R_1)/(1-t)$
implements the \emph{current-point} form
\eqref{eq:so3-cvf-scratch}, algebraically equal to $\Xi$ by
\Cref{prop:so3-current-point} but singular in floating-point arithmetic as
$t\to1$ (\Cref{rem:so3-singularity-remark}) --- again not used during
training, only at sampling time and inside the
\texttt{use\_true\_vector\_field} oracle path. The module additionally
carries an explicit inline comment recording exactly
\Cref{caution:omega-clamp}'s finding in this document's own words: the
original SigmaDock \pyfunc{Omega} clamp broke exact
$\exp\circ\log=\mathrm{id}$ round trips needed by
\eqref{eq:so3-interp-detail}, motivating the narrower clamp confirmed in
\Cref{sec:so3-log-scratch} --- i.e.\ the bugfix's rationale is documented,
first-party, directly in the file that needed it.
\end{codebox}

\begin{remark}[The hard-coded $R_1\equiv I$ simplification]
\label{rem:r1-identity-simplification}
A significant, easily-missed simplification, confirmed directly against
\file{sigma\_flow\_generator.py}::\pyfunc{get\_fragment\_com\_and\_rot}: the
function that computes each fragment's data-time state
$(\mathrm{trans}_1,R_1)$ from the reference conformer sets the rotational
component to the identity, \emph{always}, for every fragment, with an
explicit inline comment: ``Rotation matrix is identity: it is (and should
be) independent in the vector field.'' Consequently, throughout the actual
training and sampling code, $R_1\equiv I$ is not a general fragment
orientation but a fixed constant, and formulas that look like they involve
a genuine $R_1$ --- for instance the delta-rotation
$\Delta R := R_t\,R_1^\top$ appearing inside
\Cref{prop:fragment-reconstruction}'s reconstruction map when specialised
to this codebase --- collapse to $\Delta R=R_t$ exactly. This is
consistent with, and is the concrete mechanism realising,
\Cref{sec:sigmadock-symmetry-precise}'s gauge-invariance discussion: since
the reference conformer's own orientation is arbitrary
(\Cref{thm:sigmadock-symmetry}'s gauge freedom), fixing it at the identity
is simply one particular, legitimate choice of gauge, not a loss of
generality --- but a reader auditing the code without this fact stated
explicitly could easily mistake $R_1$ for a nontrivial, data-derived
quantity, which it is not.
\end{remark}

\section{No output dressing: the entire diffusion-specific scalar machinery is gone}
\label{sec:sigmaflow-nodressing}

\begin{codebox}
\textbf{Mathematical object:} the network's predicted vector field,
$(\mathrm{pred\_u\_t\_trans},\mathrm{pred\_u\_t\_R})$ \\
\textbf{Implementation:} \file{sigma\_flow\_generator.py}::\pyfunc{\_compute\_vector\_field},
quoted here in full (it is short enough to reproduce verbatim, and its
brevity is itself the point):
\begin{verbatim}
def _compute_vector_field(self, sampled, updates, t_batch):
    """
    Interprets the pooled per-fragment force/torque directly as the
    predicted conditional vector field. No time dependent scaling
    needed, unlike the diffusion score.
    """
    pred_u_t_trans = updates["total_force"]
    pred_u_t_R = updates["omega"]
    return {"pred_u_t_trans": pred_u_t_trans, "pred_u_t_R": pred_u_t_R}
\end{verbatim}
\textbf{Interpretation:} \texttt{updates["total\_force"]} and
\texttt{updates["omega"]} are exactly the raw Newton--Maruyama outputs
$dT=F_F/M_F$ and $\widehat{dW}=\widehat{I_F^{-1}\tau_F}$ of
\eqref{eq:newton-maruyama-precise} --- \emph{unchanged} from
\Cref{sec:newton-euler-precise}'s construction, since the physical
Newton--Euler reduction (net force, torque, mass, inertia) is one of the
components \Cref{tab:sigmadock-vs-sigmaflow-precise} lists as unmodified.
What is different is that this raw mechanical output is returned
\emph{directly} as the predicted vector field, with \textbf{no}
$t$-dependent rescaling of the kind
\Cref{eq:sd-head-precise,eq:sd-head-rot-space,eq:sd-head-rot-score}
apply: no \texttt{r3\_scaling}, no \texttt{so3\_scaling}, no
$\partial_\omega\log f_\sigma/\omega$ dressing, no branch selection
(\texttt{rot\_score\_method}/\texttt{rot\_score\_scaling}, whose
corresponding config fields were removed entirely from
\file{config.py} in an earlier development pass of this project, since
they had become dead code once this function stopped consulting them).
The comment's own justification --- ``no time dependent scaling needed,
unlike the diffusion score'' --- is a genuine mathematical observation, not
an oversight: \Cref{prop:so3-constant-velocity,prop:linear-path-scratch}
both establish that the flow-matching conditional targets are
\emph{constant in $t$} (unlike the diffusion score, whose scale, per
\Cref{sec:sde-perspective}'s discussion, varies enormously across noise
levels and specifically requires the $\lambda(s)$/scalar-prefactor
machinery to keep training well-conditioned) --- \textbf{but} this
observation concerns the \emph{training target's} scale, not necessarily
whether the specific quantity a fixed-capacity network can most easily
regress onto (raw Newton--Euler output, versus some other, still
$t$-independent, reparameterisation) is optimally chosen; this project's
broader experimental investigation (the ``time-weighting'' variant referenced
in this project's development history) tested, and found no benefit from,
reintroducing a $t$-dependent multiplicative reweighting on top of this
already-unweighted target, discussed further in
\Cref{sec:variant-time-weighting}.
\end{codebox}

\section{The loss function}
\label{sec:sigmaflow-loss}

\begin{codebox}
\textbf{Mathematical object:} $\mathcal L_{\mathrm{trans}}$,
$\mathcal L_R$ of \Cref{thm:cfm-equivalence-scratch,thm:rcfm-so3},
evaluated at their sampled-path targets \\
\textbf{Implementation:} \file{sigma\_flow\_generator.py}::\pyfunc{compute\_losses} \\
\textbf{Interpretation:}
\begin{align*}
\texttt{loss\_trans} &= \|\mathrm{pred\_u\_t\_trans} - u_t^{\mathrm{trans}}\|_2^2
&&\text{(sum over the 3 coordinates, per fragment; }u_t^{\mathrm{trans}}=x_1-x_0\text{ of \Cref{prop:linear-path-scratch})}\\
\texttt{loss\_R} &= \|\mathrm{pred\_u\_t\_R} - u_t^R\|_{\mathrm{Fro}}^2
&&\text{(Frobenius norm}^2\text{ of the }3\times3\text{ difference; }u_t^R=\Xi\text{ of \Cref{def:so3-interp-detail})}
\end{align*}
Both are raw per-fragment sums with \textbf{no weighting and no clamping}
inside \pyfunc{compute\_losses} itself --- confirming, precisely,
\Cref{tab:sigmadock-vs-sigmaflow-precise}'s claim that SigmaFlow's loss has
\emph{two} terms where SigmaDock's \pyfunc{compute\_losses}
(\Cref{sec:sigmadock-loss}) has \emph{four}: the two ``data-space''
($\hat x_0$-reconstruction) terms \texttt{T0}/\texttt{R0} that SigmaDock's
diffusion parameterisation computes alongside its score-matching terms have
\textbf{no analogue at all} in SigmaFlow's loss, confirmed by direct
tracing of \file{trainer.py}::\pyfunc{SigmaLightningModule.\_shared\_step},
whose total-loss combination
$\texttt{total\_loss} = \texttt{trans\_score\_weight}\cdot\texttt{loss\_trans} + \texttt{rot\_score\_weight}\cdot\texttt{loss\_R}$
consumes exactly these two keys and no others (the CLI flag names
\texttt{trans\_score\_weight}/\texttt{rot\_score\_weight} are themselves a
naming holdover from the diffusion codebase this file is shared with;
functionally, for the flow-matching path, they are ordinary fixed loss
weights on \eqref{eq:linear-path-vf-scratch}/\eqref{eq:so3-cvf-scratch}'s
regression losses, not score-matching weights in
\Cref{sec:sde-perspective}'s $\lambda(s)$ sense). Per-molecule reduction
uses \file{trainer.py}::\pyfunc{scaled\_fragmented\_loss}, structurally
identical to \Cref{sec:sigmadock-loss}'s code correspondence (shared
infrastructure, per \Cref{tab:sigmadock-vs-sigmaflow-precise}).
\end{codebox}

\section{The full training step}
\label{sec:sigmaflow-training-pipeline}

We now trace \pyfunc{SigmaFlowGenerator.forward} end to end, with every
intermediate tensor's shape as commented in the source, assembling every
piece derived above into the single computational pipeline a training
batch actually executes.

\begin{enumerate}
\item \textbf{Prepare batch, sample time.}
$t=\pyfunc{\_sample\_time}(\mathrm{batch})\in[\eps_t,1]^B$, one scalar
\emph{per molecule} in the batch (\emph{not} per fragment), via
$t=\eps_t+(1-\eps_t)\cdot\mathrm{Unif}(0,1)$, $\eps_t=\texttt{HPARAMS.general.epsilon\_t}=0.01$
--- matching \Cref{sec:sde-perspective}'s $t\sim\mathcal U(0,1)$ up to the
training-time floor $\eps_t$, whose purpose (per an explicit code comment
quoted verbatim, judged authoritative and reproduced here rather than
re-derived) is distributional consistency between training and sampling,
not any numerical-singularity avoidance: ``training's \texttt{sample\_time}
never samples $t$ below this; sampling's \texttt{t\_min} should match it
$\dots$ not related to the separate $1/(1-t)$ singularity near $t=1$'' ---
i.e.\ the network should never be \emph{queried} at a $t$ value it was
never trained on, which is a distributional/generalisation concern
entirely distinct from \Cref{rem:so3-singularity-remark}'s numerical
concern about the current-point vector-field formula near $t=1$.
\item \textbf{Get initial (data-time) states.}
$\mathrm{pos}_0,\ \mathrm{trans}_1,\ R_1,\ \mathrm{num\_fragments}
= \pyfunc{\_get\_initial\_states}(\mathrm{batch})$: despite the
subscript ``$0$'', $\mathrm{pos}_0$ is the (pocket-centred,
\texttt{dimensional\_scale}-rescaled, \Cref{rem:dimensional-scale}) ground
truth/reference atom geometry --- the fixed per-fragment \emph{template}
$x^{\mathrm{ref}}$ of \Cref{prop:fragment-reconstruction}, reused at every
$t$, not a draw from the source distribution --- while $\mathrm{trans}_1,R_1$
are the per-fragment data-time (centre-of-mass, and, per
\Cref{rem:r1-identity-simplification}, identically-$I$) rigid-motion state.
$t$ is broadcast from one value per molecule to one value per fragment,
$t_{\mathrm{batch}}\in[\eps_t,1]^{\Sigma F}$ (\emph{all fragments of a
given molecule share the same sampled $t$}, a design choice with no
counterpart requirement in the theory of
\Cref{chap:flow-matching,chap:riemannian-fm}, which permit --- but do not
require --- an independent $t$ per component; the code's own inline
comment flags this exact choice as a documented, unresolved limitation:
``better sampling. Uniform makes noises (sigmas) that are too
redundant'').
\item \textbf{Sample the conditional path.}
$(\mathrm{trans}_t,R_t,u_t^{\mathrm{trans}},u_t^R) = \pyfunc{\_sample\_flow}(\mathrm{trans}_1,R_1,t_{\mathrm{batch}})$,
i.e.\ \Cref{sec:sigmaflow-translation,sec:sigmaflow-rotation}'s
\pyfunc{conditional\_probability\_path}, dispatched componentwise per
\Cref{prop:se3-product}.
\item \textbf{Reconstruct atom positions at time $t$.}
$\mathrm{pos}_t = \varphi(\mathrm{trans}_t,R_t)$ via
\Cref{prop:fragment-reconstruction}'s formula
(\pyfunc{\_apply\_transformations}), and the graph's transient (interaction)
edges are recomputed at these new positions
(\pyfunc{\_update\_batch}, \Cref{sec:sigmadock-graph}'s discussion of the
global/transient topology split).
\item \textbf{Network forward pass.}
$\mathrm{lig\_pseudoforces},\mathrm{forces\_idxs} = \pyfunc{\_compute\_forces}(\mathrm{batch},t)$
--- exactly one EquiformerV2 evaluation
(\Cref{chap:equiformer,sec:newton-euler-precise}'s code correspondence),
identical in cost and mechanism to SigmaDock's own single-network-call
Newton--Euler pipeline.
\item \textbf{Newton--Euler reduction and Newton--Maruyama update.}
$(F_F,\tau_F,M_F,I_F) = \pyfunc{\_compute\_fragment\_dynamics}(\dots)$
(\eqref{eq:newton-euler-precise}); $(dT,\widehat{dW})=\pyfunc{\_predict\_fragment\_updates}(\dots)$
(\eqref{eq:newton-maruyama-precise}) --- structurally byte-for-byte the
same construction as \Cref{sec:newton-euler-precise}, confirming
\Cref{tab:sigmadock-vs-sigmaflow-precise}'s claim that this component is
unchanged.
\item \textbf{Predicted vector field.}
$(\mathrm{pred\_u\_t\_trans},\mathrm{pred\_u\_t\_R}) = \pyfunc{\_compute\_vector\_field}(\dots)$,
\Cref{sec:sigmaflow-nodressing}'s undressed pass-through.
\item \textbf{Loss.} \Cref{sec:sigmaflow-loss}'s two-term
\pyfunc{compute\_losses}, weighted and summed by
\pyfunc{trainer.py}::\pyfunc{SigmaLightningModule.\_shared\_step}, wrapped
in the try/except-\texttt{AssertionError} and finite-value guards this
project's own development history documents in detail (catching
\Cref{def:fragment-mass-precise}'s ``fragment mass $<2$'' assertion
specifically, among other numerical failure modes).
\end{enumerate}

Every step above, except step 8's loss formula and steps 2--3's use of
the flow-matching (rather than diffusion) conditional path, is verified,
in \Cref{tab:sigmadock-vs-sigmaflow-precise}, to be identical to
SigmaDock's corresponding pipeline stage --- the precise, exhaustive sense
in which this project's conversion of SigmaDock into SigmaFlow is, as
claimed throughout this document's audited source material, a
\emph{surgical}, minimally-invasive substitution of the probability path
and training target alone.

\section{Sampling}
\label{sec:sigmaflow-sampling}

\begin{codebox}
\textbf{Mathematical object:} the forward ODE integration loop of
\Cref{sec:cfm-sampling-scratch,sec:fm-so3-scratch} \\
\textbf{Implementation:} \file{sampling.py}::\pyfunc{sampler} (in
\file{SigmaFlow\_Development/src/sigmadock/diff/}) \\
\textbf{Interpretation:} default \texttt{num\_steps}$=25$,
\texttt{t\_min}$=\eps_t=0.01$ (matching training's floor, per this
chapter's Step 1 discussion above), \texttt{rho}$=3.0$, default
\texttt{discretization="power"} (\emph{not} SigmaDock's default
\texttt{"edm"} --- a genuine, code-confirmed default-configuration
asymmetry between the two samplers, flagged here rather than assumed
identical: any timing/quality comparison between the two must either
override one to match the other or explicitly account for this
difference), \texttt{solver="euler"} (Heun again unimplemented, per
\Cref{sec:sigmadock-sampling}'s finding, verbatim-identical dead
\texttt{\# TODO} comment block in this file too). The timestep array walks
\emph{upward}, $t_{\min}\to t_{\max}=1$, matching this document's global
convention directly (no relabelling needed, unlike SigmaDock's sampler).
Each loop iteration: (1) evaluate the closed-form current-point field
$u_t(\cdot\mid z)$ (\pyfunc{\_compute\_true\_vector\_field}, i.e.\
\Cref{eq:linear-path-vf-scratch,eq:so3-cvf-scratch}'s singular-but-exact
forms, using the \emph{known} $\mathrm{trans}_1,R_1$ --- available here
because sampling is being run, in this project's own evaluation
methodology, on complexes whose true pose is known, purely for
diagnostic loss logging at each step, not because a real blind-docking
deployment would have this available); (2) if
\texttt{use\_true\_vector\_field=False} (the normal, non-diagnostic case),
run the network (Steps 5--7 of \Cref{sec:sigmaflow-training-pipeline}'s
list) to get the predicted field instead; (3) Euler step
(\pyfunc{SE3\_FlowMatcher.euler\_step}, dispatching to
\Cref{sec:sigmaflow-translation,sec:sigmaflow-rotation}'s componentwise
\pyfunc{euler\_step} implementations); (4) reconstruct positions via
$\varphi$ (\Cref{prop:fragment-reconstruction}) at the new
$(\mathrm{trans}_{t+dt},R_{t+dt})$. Exactly one network evaluation per
step, matching SigmaDock's sampler exactly in this specific respect
(\Cref{sec:sigmadock-sampling}).
\end{codebox}

\begin{remark}[The \texttt{use\_true\_vector\_field} oracle: what Test~1 established]
\label{rem:oracle-test-recap}
Substituting the closed-form field for the network's prediction at
\emph{every} step (\texttt{use\_true\_vector\_field=True}) isolates a
question this chapter's derivations make precise: does the ODE
integration/reconstruction machinery itself (Euler stepping, the
$\varphi$ reconstruction, batching and edge recomputation) introduce error,
independent of network quality? By \Cref{prop:so3-current-point}'s exact
algebraic identity and \Cref{prop:linear-path-scratch}'s exact linearity
(both established rigorously in this chapter, not merely observed
empirically), the answer, \emph{in exact arithmetic}, must be no: the
oracle field reconstructs the true trajectory exactly, for any number of
steps, since both conditional vector fields are (by
\Cref{sec:cfm-sampling-scratch}'s remark on the linear path, and
\Cref{prop:so3-current-point} for the geodesic path) either exactly
integrable by a single Euler step (translation) or exactly consistent with
the constant-velocity geodesic the Euler step is discretising (rotation,
up to floating-point round-off). This is the theoretical content
underlying this project's empirical ``Test 1'' finding (oracle-vector-field
sampling reconstructs the true structure to within floating-point
precision, $\le0.0002$\,\r A deviation, across a sample of real generated
structures) --- the theorem, derived here, is what the experiment
confirmed, not a separate, independent fact.
\end{remark}

\section{Likelihood and confidence: what is implemented, and what is not}
\label{sec:likelihood-confidence}

\Cref{sec:cnf-likelihood} derived, from first principles, that a CNF's
log-density along any trajectory evolves by
$\frac{d}{dt}\log p_t(X_t) = -(\nabla\cdot u_t)(X_t)$
(\Cref{cor:instantaneous-cov}), estimable via the Hutchinson trace
estimator without a separate density network. This is a genuine,
well-established capability of the flow-matching framework in general (and
one diffusion models do not share as directly, since the diffusion
side's analogous likelihood requires the full probability-flow-ODE
construction of \Cref{thm:pf-ode-scratch}, an extra derived object rather
than something immediately available from the sampler already being run).

\begin{caution}[Not implemented in this codebase]
\label{caution:likelihood-not-implemented}
Per the code-research pass underlying this document, \textbf{no code path
in either \file{sampling.py} or \file{sigma\_flow\_generator.py} computes
$\nabla\cdot u_\theta$, a Hutchinson trace estimate, or any accumulated
log-density along a sampled trajectory}; no likelihood or confidence score
derived from \Cref{cor:instantaneous-cov} is produced anywhere in the
sampling pipeline reconstructed in \Cref{sec:sigmaflow-sampling}. SigmaFlow's
actual ranking mechanism, where used, is inherited unchanged from
SigmaDock's non-learned heuristic (\Cref{sec:sigmadock-ranking}), per
\Cref{tab:sigmadock-vs-sigmaflow-precise}. \Cref{cor:instantaneous-cov}'s
construction is recorded in this document because it is a genuine,
theoretically well-founded \emph{capability} that the flow-matching
formulation --- unlike the diffusion formulation --- would make relatively
straightforward to add (a scalar ODE run alongside the existing vector
field, using an already-trained $u_\theta$ with no retraining required),
and because \Cref{chap:docking}'s discussion of confidence estimation as a
distinct task from pose generation
(\Cref{sec:docking-tasks}) specifically named this as an open, unimplemented
direction; it should not be read as a description of current SigmaFlow
behaviour.
\end{caution}

\section{A tested, and rejected, loss variant}
\label{sec:variant-time-weighting}

We record, briefly and for completeness given
\Cref{sec:sigmaflow-nodressing}'s discussion of the ``no time-dependent
scaling needed'' design choice, one concrete alternative this project's
broader experimental work tested empirically, since a reader who has just
followed \Cref{sec:sde-perspective}'s discussion of diffusion's
$\lambda(s)$ weighting might reasonably ask whether an analogous
reweighting should be added to SigmaFlow's (currently unweighted, per
\Cref{sec:sigmaflow-loss}) flow-matching loss.

\begin{caution}[Empirically tested, no benefit found; not part of this document's core theory]
\label{caution:time-weighting-empirical}
A variant multiplying \Cref{sec:sigmaflow-loss}'s per-fragment squared
errors by a factor $1/(1-t)^2$ (capped near $t=1$ for numerical stability)
was implemented, trained, and evaluated as part of this project's broader
experimental investigation. Two findings from that investigation are
relevant here, stated precisely rather than left vague: \textbf{(i)}
this reweighting has \emph{no} grounding in the flow-matching theory
developed in \Cref{chap:flow-matching,chap:riemannian-fm} of this document
--- unlike diffusion's $\lambda(s)$, which \Cref{sec:sde-perspective} derives
as compensating for the conditional score's genuinely $t$-varying scale,
\Cref{sec:sigmaflow-nodressing}'s constant-in-$t$ conditional targets have
no analogous scale variation to compensate for, so this variant is, by this
document's own theory, an unmotivated modification rather than a
theoretically indicated correction; \textbf{(ii)} consistent with point
(i), it was found, empirically, to produce no measurable improvement (and
if anything a marginal degradation) in the fragment-boundary bond-length
metric this project's broader work uses to quantify exactly the failure
mode \Cref{sec:triangulation-precise}'s discussion anticipates. We record
this only to close the loop on a natural question the theory of this
chapter raises, not as part of SigmaFlow's actual, shipped training
recipe.
\end{caution}

\clearpage

% ================================================================
\chapter{The Output Heads, Audited}
\label{chap:output-heads}

This chapter answers one question for each quantity the generative process
consumes: \emph{what is this object, in which space does it live, in which
frame is it expressed, and how must it transform under a global rotation?}

The method is deliberately narrow. No frame label is taken from a comment, a
variable name, or an earlier passage of this document --- several of those
have already been shown to disagree with each other. Every claim below is
first derived from the operations the code actually performs, and then
confirmed numerically by applying a random $Q\in SO(3)$ to a real input graph
and comparing the two sides of the candidate transformation law. Where the
derivation and the measurement disagree, the measurement decides what the
code does, and the disagreement is recorded rather than smoothed over.

\begin{caution}[Three heads were expected; the code has one]
\label{caution:one-head-not-three}
The natural mental model of a rigid-fragment docking network --- one head for
translation, one for rotation, one for torsion --- does not describe
SigmaDock or SigmaFlow. \pyfunc{EquiformerV2.forward} has exactly one
geometric output:
\[
\texttt{forces}\in\RR^{N\times3},
\]
one $3$-vector per \emph{atom}. Translation and rotation are not predicted;
they are \emph{derived} from that single field by classical rigid-body
mechanics, outside the network. Torsion is not predicted at all. The rest of
this chapter is organised around that fact.
\end{caution}

\section{Locating the heads}
\label{sec:heads-locate}

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lll}
\toprule
Output & Where it is produced & Kind \\
\midrule
per-atom pseudo-force & \file{net/model.py}::\pyfunc{EquiformerV2.forward} & network \\
translation & \file{diff/sigma\_flow\_generator.py}::\pyfunc{linear\_mechanics} & derived \\
rotation & same, then \pyfunc{newton\_maruyama}, then \pyfunc{\_compute\_vector\_field} & derived \\
torsion & --- & does not exist \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

A repository-wide search for \texttt{torsion} and \texttt{dihedral} returns
$160$ hits, all of them in \file{chem/} (fragmentation and preprocessing),
\file{config.py}, \file{data.py}, and one comment in \file{net/\_\_init\_\_.py}
labelling edge type $3$. In \file{diff/} the count is zero, and in
\file{net/} the only occurrence is that comment. There is no torsion head to
audit.

\begin{remark}[What replaced the torsion head]
\label{rem:torsion-is-fragmentation}
Torsional flexibility has not been dropped; it has been moved out of the
model and into the state space. \pyfunc{detect\_torsional\_bonds} cuts the
ligand at its rotatable bonds into $F$ rigid fragments, and each fragment
carries its own $SE(3)$ element. A torsion about a cut bond is then not an
angle the network predicts, but the \emph{relative} rigid motion of the two
fragments on either side --- already representable by the existing per-fragment
outputs. This is why the state space is $SE(3)^F$ and not
$SE(3)\times\mathbb T^k$: the torsional degrees of freedom were converted
into rigid-body ones at the data-processing stage.

The price is that bond lengths and angles across a cut are no longer
constrained by construction, which is exactly what the triangulation prior of
\Cref{chap:sigmadock} exists to repair.
\end{remark}

\section{The tensor entering the heads}
\label{sec:heads-input}

Only one tensor leaves the backbone.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{ll}
\toprule
Property & Value \\
\midrule
Object & \pyfunc{SO3\_Embedding}, attribute \texttt{.embedding} \\
Shape & \shape{N, (L+1)\textasciicircum 2, C} $=$ \shape{N, 16, 128} at defaults \\
Level & node (per atom); edges are consumed, not returned \\
$(\ell,m)$ layout & flattened, $\ell$-major: index $\ell^2+\ell+m$ \\
$L$ & \texttt{l\_max\_list} $=(3,)$ \\
$m$ cap & \texttt{m\_max\_list} $=(2,)$, applied in the \emph{edge} frame only \\
Frame & global (world); the edge frame exists only inside a block \\
Last op before readout & \pyfunc{self.norm}, then \pyfunc{self.force\_block} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

The index map $\ell^2+\ell+m$ gives, for $L=3$:

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lccccc}
\toprule
$\ell$ & $m$ range & indices & count & running total & role \\
\midrule
$0$ & $0$ & $0$ & $1$ & $1$ & scalars \\
$1$ & $-1..1$ & $1,2,3$ & $3$ & $4$ & \textbf{the readout} \\
$2$ & $-2..2$ & $4..8$ & $5$ & $9$ & internal only \\
$3$ & $-3..3$ & $9..15$ & $7$ & $16$ & internal only \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\pyfunc{force\_block} is a further \pyfunc{SO2EquivariantGraphAttention}
--- a full attention block, not a linear layer --- configured with one output
channel. Its output is sliced by \texttt{narrow(1, 1, 3)}: axis $1$, offset
$1$, length $3$, i.e.\ exactly indices $1,2,3$, exactly $\ell=1$. Degrees
$\ell=0,2,3$ are computed and then discarded at the very last step.

\section{Translation}
\label{sec:head-translation}

\paragraph{Mathematical requirement.} A translational velocity is a polar
vector: under a global rotation of the scene it must satisfy
$v(Qx)=Q\,v(x)$.

\paragraph{What the code does.} \pyfunc{linear\_mechanics} sums the per-atom
field over each fragment, and \pyfunc{newton\_maruyama} divides by the atom
count:
\[
F_f=\sum_{i\in f}f_i,
\qquad
v_f^{\mathrm{tr}}=\frac{F_f}{m_f},\qquad m_f=|f| .
\]
That is the whole translation head. There is no projection layer, no
magnitude/direction factorisation, no separate $\ell=0$ scaling, and no
learned pooling --- an unweighted sum and a division by an integer. In
particular the answer to ``is $v$ built as $s\,u/\|u\|$ from an $\ell=0$
magnitude and a normalised $\ell=1$ direction?'' is \textbf{no}, for
translation and for rotation alike. Every head in this codebase is linear in
the per-atom field.

The centre of mass enters only through \pyfunc{get\_fragment\_com\_and\_rot},
and only as the pivot for the torque; the net force does not use it.

\begin{codebox}
\textbf{Where does it live?} \\
$v^{\mathrm{tr}}_f\in\RR^3$, one per fragment. \\
\textbf{Frame:} world, the same Cartesian frame as the input coordinates
(after pocket-COM subtraction and division by
\texttt{dimensional\_scale}). \\
\textbf{Transformation law:} $v^{\mathrm{tr}}(Qx)=Q\,v^{\mathrm{tr}}(x)$. \\
\textbf{Measured:} $4.5\times10^{-15}$ relative, float64
(\Cref{tab:head-residuals}).
\end{codebox}

\section{Rotation}
\label{sec:head-rotation}

This is the head that matters, and the one where the labels in the source
have historically been wrong.

\subsection{The two angular velocities, restated next to the code}

For a curve $R(\tau)$ in $SO(3)$ there are two ways to write the tangent, and
they are distinguished by the \emph{equation}, never by a label:
\begin{align}
\dot R &= R\,\widehat{\omega^{\mathrm{bd}}}
&&\text{body coordinates,} \label{eq:head-body}\\
\dot R &= \widehat{\omega^{\mathrm{sp}}}\,R
&&\text{spatial (world) coordinates,} \label{eq:head-spatial}
\end{align}
related by
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:head-adjoint}
\omega^{\mathrm{sp}} = R\,\omega^{\mathrm{bd}},
\qquad\text{equivalently}\qquad
\widehat{\omega^{\mathrm{sp}}} = R\,\widehat{\omega^{\mathrm{bd}}}\,R^{\mathsf T}.
\end{equation}
The second form of \eqref{eq:head-adjoint} is the one the code uses, because
it stores $\widehat\omega$, not $\omega$. Conjugation by a rotation preserves
skew-symmetry, so both sides are genuine $\so(3)$ elements.

Two consumption sites in SigmaFlow fix which of
\eqref{eq:head-body}--\eqref{eq:head-spatial} the loss and the sampler expect,
and they agree:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lll}
\toprule
Site & Code & Implies \\
\midrule
target & \pyfunc{calc\_rot\_vector\_field}: $\log(R_t^{\mathsf T}R_1)/(1-t)$ & body \\
integrator & \pyfunc{euler\_step}: \texttt{R\_t @ exp(v\_t*dt)} & body \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
$R_{\text{next}}=R_t\exp(v\,dt)$ is right multiplication, i.e.\ $\dot R=Rv$,
i.e.\ \eqref{eq:head-body}. The docstring of \pyfunc{calc\_rot\_vector\_field}
calls this ``right trivialised''; \Cref{sec:so3-trivialisation} of this
document calls the same object left-trivialised. Both names are in
circulation and neither is used here as evidence --- \eqref{eq:head-body} is
the definition, the label is not.

\subsection{The trace, operation by operation}

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{llll}
\toprule
\# & Operation & Code & Shape \\
\midrule
1 & backbone features & \pyfunc{blocks}, \pyfunc{norm} & \shape{N,16,128} \\
2 & attention readout & \pyfunc{force\_block} & \shape{N,16,1} \\
3 & select $\ell=1$ & \texttt{narrow(1,1,3)} & \shape{N,3,1} \\
4 & flatten & \texttt{view(-1,3)} & \shape{N,3} \\
5 & sign flip, ligand select & \pyfunc{\_compute\_forces}: \texttt{-eps[idx]} & \shape{N\_L,3} \\
6 & torque about COM & $\tau_f=\sum_{i\in f}(x_i-c_f)\times f_i$ & \shape{F,3} \\
7 & inertia solve & $\Delta W=I_f^{-1}\tau_f$ & \shape{F,3} \\
8 & hat map & $\widehat{\Delta W}$, then clamp to $\pm10^3$ & \shape{F,3,3} \\
9 & \textbf{frame conversion} & $R_t^{\mathsf T}\,\widehat{\Delta W}\,R_t$ & \shape{F,3,3} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

Step 9 is the only place a rotation matrix touches the rotational output. It
lives in \pyfunc{\_compute\_vector\_field} and it is the ``frame fix''. It
happens \emph{after} aggregation, on the per-fragment quantity, and it uses
$R_t$ --- the flow-matching path variable --- not an edge frame and not a
learned rotation.

Reading \eqref{eq:head-adjoint} right to left: steps 6--8 produce
$\widehat{\omega^{\mathrm{sp}}}$, and step 9 computes
$R_t^{\mathsf T}\widehat{\omega^{\mathrm{sp}}}R_t=\widehat{\omega^{\mathrm{bd}}}$.
So the head does produce the body-frame quantity that steps ``target'' and
``integrator'' above require. The derivation and the code agree.

\subsection{$\RR^3$ or $\so(3)$?}

Both, at different points, and the code changes representation twice:
\begin{itemize}
\item Steps 6--7 hold $\tau_f,\Delta W\in\RR^3$: three numbers, an ordinary
      Cartesian triple.
\item Step 8 applies the hat map
      $\widehat{\cdot}:\RR^3\to\so(3)$, a linear isomorphism onto the
      skew-symmetric $3\times3$ matrices, with $\widehat v\,w=v\times w$.
\item From step 9 onward the object is a matrix in $\so(3)$, and it stays a
      matrix: \pyfunc{compute\_losses} takes a Frobenius norm over the last
      two axes, and \pyfunc{euler\_step} feeds it to the matrix exponential.
\end{itemize}
So the \emph{stored} object is a $3\times3$ matrix, and the loss is computed
on matrices. Since $\|\widehat a-\widehat b\|_F^2=2\|a-b\|_2^2$, the matrix
Frobenius loss equals twice the vector loss --- a constant factor, absorbed
into the loss weight, but worth knowing when comparing loss magnitudes across
implementations.

\subsection{Rotation or rotation rate?}

The head does not predict $R_{\text{new}}$. Every quantity from step 6 onward
lives in a tangent space: $\so(3)$, the Lie algebra, not the group. The
network output is a \emph{rate}, integrated by
$R_{\text{next}}=R_t\exp(v\,dt)$. It is only a \emph{velocity} rather than a
score because of how the target is built --- and that distinction, together
with the units and scaling question, is the subject of the next round, not
this one.

\subsection{The group action is conjugation, not left multiplication}
\label{sec:head-group-action}

Here the standard textbook statement and this codebase part company, and the
difference is not cosmetic.

\paragraph{Mathematical requirement.} If a global rotation acts on fragment
orientations by $R\mapsto QR$, then a body-frame angular velocity is
\emph{invariant}: from \eqref{eq:head-body},
$\dot R\mapsto Q\dot R$ and $R\mapsto QR$ give
$\widehat{\omega^{\mathrm{bd}}}=R^{\mathsf T}\dot R\mapsto
R^{\mathsf T}Q^{\mathsf T}Q\dot R=R^{\mathsf T}\dot R$,
unchanged. A spatial one transforms as
$\omega^{\mathrm{sp}}\mapsto Q\omega^{\mathrm{sp}}$. That is the standard
dichotomy, and it is what this document asserted before this audit.

\paragraph{What SigmaFlow actually implements.} $R_t$ is not a fragment's
absolute orientation. It is a \emph{delta} rotation relative to the reference
structure \texttt{pos\_0}, applied about the fragment COM by
\pyfunc{get\_transformations\_from\_rototranslations}:
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:head-pose-map}
x^{(t)}_i = \Delta R_f\,(x^{(0)}_i-c_f) + c_f + \Delta T_f,
\qquad \Delta R_f = R_t R_1^{\mathsf T} .
\end{equation}
Two facts about \eqref{eq:head-pose-map} settle the group action:
\begin{enumerate}
\item \texttt{pos\_0} is an \emph{input} to the network --- it is read by
      \pyfunc{compute\_edge\_embedding} for the boundary edges --- so a global
      rotation of the scene rotates it too: $x^{(0)}\mapsto Qx^{(0)}$,
      $c_f\mapsto Qc_f$.
\item \pyfunc{get\_fragment\_com\_and\_rot} returns $R_1=I_3$
      \emph{unconditionally}, with the comment ``Rotation matrix is identity:
      it is (and should be) independent in the vector field.''
\end{enumerate}
Substituting $x^{(0)}\mapsto Qx^{(0)}$, $c_f\mapsto Qc_f$ into
\eqref{eq:head-pose-map} and demanding $x^{(t)}\mapsto Qx^{(t)}$ forces
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:head-conjugation}
\boxed{\;R_t\;\longmapsto\;Q\,R_t\,Q^{\mathsf T}\;}
\end{equation}
and not $R_t\mapsto QR_t$. This is also the only action compatible with
$R_1\equiv I$: conjugation fixes the identity, $QIQ^{\mathsf T}=I$, whereas
left multiplication does not, $QI=Q\ne I$.

Measured directly against \pyfunc{\_apply\_transformations}, asking which
substitution reproduces $Q\,x^{(t)}$:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lr}
\toprule
Substitution & relative residual \\
\midrule
$R_t\mapsto QR_t$ & $1.06$ \\
$R_t\mapsto QR_tQ^{\mathsf T}$ & $3.7\times10^{-16}$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\begin{proposition}[The body-frame output is \emph{not} invariant here]
\label{prop:head-not-invariant}
Under \eqref{eq:head-conjugation},
\[
\widehat{\omega^{\mathrm{bd}}}
= R_t^{\mathsf T}\widehat{\omega^{\mathrm{sp}}}R_t
\;\longmapsto\;
(QR_tQ^{\mathsf T})^{\mathsf T}\,(Q\widehat{\omega^{\mathrm{sp}}}Q^{\mathsf T})\,(QR_tQ^{\mathsf T})
= Q\,\bigl(R_t^{\mathsf T}\widehat{\omega^{\mathrm{sp}}}R_t\bigr)\,Q^{\mathsf T},
\]
i.e.\ it transforms by conjugation, equivalently
$\omega^{\mathrm{bd}}\mapsto Q\,\omega^{\mathrm{bd}}$ after the vee map.
It is \textbf{not} left unchanged.
\end{proposition}

\begin{caution}[Correction to \Cref{sec:two-requirements}]
\label{caution:head-correction}
An earlier passage of this document stated that ``equivariance of the
body-frame rotational output under a world-frame rotation of the whole scene
means the body-frame output is left \emph{unchanged}, since it is expressed
relative to the fragment's own, co-rotating frame''. That is correct for the
action $R\mapsto QR$ on an \emph{absolute} orientation. It is false for
SigmaFlow, whose $R_t$ is a delta rotation against a co-rotating reference
and therefore conjugates. Measured: the invariance hypothesis fails at
$1.20$ relative, the conjugation hypothesis holds at $2.3\times10^{-15}$.

The lesson generalises: ``body-frame'' fixes the relation between the tangent
vector and $\dot R$; it does \emph{not} by itself determine the
transformation law, because that also depends on how the group acts on $R$.
Both must be stated.
\end{caution}

Crucially, the target transforms the same way. With
$u_t^R=\log(R_t^{\mathsf T}R_1)/(1-t)$ and $R_1=I$ preserved by conjugation,
$u_t^R\mapsto Q\,u_t^R\,Q^{\mathsf T}$ --- measured $8.3\times10^{-15}$.
Prediction and target therefore transform identically, the Frobenius norm of
their difference is invariant, and the loss is a well-defined function on the
quotient. \textbf{The frame fix is correct.}

\begin{codebox}
\textbf{Where does it live?} \\
$\widehat{\omega^{\mathrm{bd}}}\in\so(3)$, stored as \shape{F,3,3};
equivalently $\omega^{\mathrm{bd}}\in\RR^3$ via the vee map. \\
\textbf{Frame:} body, in the sense $\dot R=R\widehat\omega$
\eqref{eq:head-body} --- fixed by \pyfunc{euler\_step}'s right
multiplication, not by any comment. \\
\textbf{Transformation law:}
$\widehat\omega\mapsto Q\widehat\omega Q^{\mathsf T}$ under
$R_t\mapsto QR_tQ^{\mathsf T}$, \emph{not} invariant. \\
\textbf{Measured:} $2.3\times10^{-15}$ relative, float64.
\end{codebox}

\section{Numerical verification}
\label{sec:head-numerics}

\paragraph{Setup.} Two real complexes from the dummy set, $N=887$ nodes,
$E=4564$ edges; randomly initialised weights with
\texttt{zero\_init\_last=False} (the default \texttt{True} zeroes the final
layer, so the network would output exactly $0$ and every equivariance test
would pass vacuously); \pyfunc{eval} mode, all dropout at $0$; float64
throughout the geometric path; $12$ Haar-random rotations per row; residuals
are $\max|a-b|/\max(\|a\|_\infty,\|b\|_\infty)$.

Equivariance is a property of the architecture, not of the weights, so no
trained checkpoint is needed --- and a trained one would prove less, since a
low loss is not evidence of a correct frame.

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\begin{tabular}{llr}
\toprule
Quantity & Hypothesis tested & Residual \\
\midrule
\texttt{forces} & $f(Qx)=Q\,f(x)$ & $5.3\times10^{-15}$ \\
\texttt{forces} & $f(Qx)=f(x)$ & $1.47$ \\
\texttt{forces} & $f(Qx)=PQP^{\mathsf T}f(x)$ & $1.49$ \\
\midrule
\texttt{total\_force} & $F\mapsto QF$ & $4.5\times10^{-15}$ \\
\texttt{total\_torque} & $\tau\mapsto Q\tau$ & $1.3\times10^{-15}$ \\
inertia & $I\mapsto QIQ^{\mathsf T}$ & $8.6\times10^{-16}$ \\
\texttt{delta\_W} & $\Delta W\mapsto Q\,\Delta W$ & $2.4\times10^{-15}$ \\
\texttt{omega} & $\widehat\omega\mapsto Q\widehat\omega Q^{\mathsf T}$ & $2.6\times10^{-15}$ \\
\midrule
\texttt{pred\_u\_t\_R} & invariant & $1.20$ \\
\texttt{pred\_u\_t\_R} & $u\mapsto QuQ^{\mathsf T}$ & $2.3\times10^{-15}$ \\
\texttt{u\_t\_R} (target) & $u\mapsto QuQ^{\mathsf T}$ & $8.3\times10^{-15}$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Frame audit. Rows use the gate activation, which is exactly
equivariant; see \Cref{sec:head-s2-caveat} for the production configuration.
$P$ is the permutation $xyz\mapsto yzx$.}
\label{tab:head-residuals}
\end{table}

\paragraph{The permutation hypothesis, and why it had to be tested.}
In the \texttt{e3nn} convention the real $\ell=1$ spherical harmonics are
ordered $(m=-1,0,+1)\propto(y,z,x)$, not $(x,y,z)$. Had the readout inherited
that ordering while the mechanics downstream read the three numbers as
Cartesian $xyz$, the composed map would transform as $PQP^{\mathsf T}$ and
the bug would be nearly invisible: the output would still be a $3$-vector of
plausible magnitude, and the loss would still decrease. It is refuted at
$1.49$.

The reason it is refuted closes a loop left open earlier: \pyfunc{init\_edge\_rot\_mat}
builds \texttt{rot\_inv} with columns $[n_z,n_x,n_y]$, aligning the edge to
$e_2$ --- the \emph{second} axis. In the $(y,z,x)$ ordering the second slot is
exactly the $m=0$ slot, which is what the eSCN reduction requires. The
apparently odd choice of $e_2$ over $e_1$ and the apparently odd $(y,z,x)$
ordering are the same convention, and they cancel. What last round recorded
as a bare empirical fact now has its reason.

\section{A defect found on the way}
\label{sec:head-s2-caveat}

The first pass of this audit produced residuals near $10^{-1}$ for
\emph{every} hypothesis, too noisy to separate ``exact'' from
``approximate''. The cause turned out to be worth reporting in its own right.

\pyfunc{init\_edge\_rot\_mat} chooses the axis perpendicular to each edge by
\texttt{torch.rand\_like}, freshly, on every forward pass. The eSCN
construction is supposed to be indifferent to that choice: different choices
differ by a rotation about the edge axis, and the $SO(2)$ convolution
commutes with it. Measured on the production configuration
($128$ channels, $6$ layers, $L=3$, $m_{\max}=2$, $14.98$\,M parameters), it
is not indifferent:

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lrr}
\toprule
Configuration & gauge dependence & equivariance error \\
\midrule
S$^2$ activation (production default) & $1.06\times10^{-1}$ & $9.4\times10^{-2}$ \\
gate activation & $1.4\times10^{-15}$ & $5.3\times10^{-15}$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

Swapping only the activation moves both numbers by fourteen orders of
magnitude, which localises the defect precisely. In
\pyfunc{SO2EquivariantGraphAttention.forward} the S$^2$ activation sits at
line~$270$, between \texttt{\_rotate} (line~$250$) and \texttt{\_rotate\_inv}
(line~$324$) --- that is, \emph{inside the edge frame}. It evaluates features
on a fixed grid on the sphere, and that grid is not invariant under rotation
about the edge axis. The arbitrary azimuthal choice therefore moves the
features relative to the grid, and the discretisation error does not cancel.

Two consequences follow, and neither is a rounding issue:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{The forward pass is not deterministic.} In \pyfunc{eval} mode,
      with all dropout disabled, two evaluations of the identical input differ
      by about $10\%$ relative. During ODE sampling a fresh gauge is drawn at
      every step.
\item \textbf{$SO(3)$ equivariance is broken at the same order.} About $10\%$,
      in the shipped configuration.
\end{itemize}

\begin{caution}[Scope, and what is \emph{not} claimed]
\label{caution:s2-scope}
These numbers are measured at \textbf{random initialisation}. Whether
training suppresses the effect --- by learning features that are smooth enough
on the grid for aliasing to be small --- is not established here, and cannot
be: the only checkpoints available locally are $15$-step archives. Measuring
this on a trained checkpoint is marked \texttt{PENDING ARC VALIDATION}.

It is also not claimed that this explains the rotation-channel failure. It is
a quantified, reproducible defect in the shipped configuration, on the path
the rotational output travels, and it is a candidate --- nothing stronger
until it is measured on trained weights. Note also that it affects SigmaDock
and SigmaFlow \emph{identically}, since both use the same backbone, so it
cannot by itself explain a \emph{difference} between them.
\end{caution}

\section{Open items}
\label{sec:head-open}

Deliberately left for the next round, to keep this one narrow:
\begin{itemize}
\item S$^2$ aliasing on a trained checkpoint --- \texttt{PENDING ARC VALIDATION}.
\item Score-versus-velocity semantics, units, and the
      \texttt{dimensional\_scale} bookkeeping.
\item Whether the $\pm10^3$ clamp on $\widehat{\Delta W}$ in
      \pyfunc{newton\_maruyama} ever binds in practice, and what it does to
      the gradient when it does.
\item The inertia eigenvalue clamp, likewise.

\chapter{Backbone, Mechanics, and Generative Semantics}
\label{chap:engine-swap}

The previous chapter established what the network emits. This one asks a
different question: \emph{which parts of the code belong to the geometry,
which to the diffusion process, which were changed for flow matching --- and
are the changes mathematically coherent?}

The organising device is a strict three-layer split. Confusing the layers is
what makes ``score'' and ``velocity'' look interchangeable when they are not.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{clll}
\toprule
& Layer & Content & Status \\
\midrule
A & geometric backbone & graph, embeddings, EquiformerV2, $\ell=1$ readout & byte-identical \\
B & mechanical reduction & force $\to$ torque $\to$ inertia $\to$ $\omega$ & formula-identical \\
C & generative semantics & path, target, loss, sampler & entirely replaced \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\section{Layer A is byte-identical}
\label{sec:engine-layer-a}

Not ``similar'': identical. A file-by-file \texttt{diff} of the backbone
between \file{SigmaDock/src\_sigmadock\_net/} and
\file{SigmaFlow\_Minimal/src/sigmadock/net/} returns zero differing lines for
all seventeen files, \file{model.py} included --- so the graph construction,
the featurisation, the attention, the normalisation, the $S^2$ activation and
the readout are the same code, and the trainable parameter count is
identical by construction.

Outside \file{net/}, the only differences in the shared pipeline are
non-semantic:

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lll}
\toprule
File & Differing lines & Nature \\
\midrule
\file{net/*} (17 files) & $0$ & --- \\
\file{chem/processing.py} & $0$ & --- \\
\file{oracle.py} & $5$ & one comment \\
\file{data.py} & $13$ & error reporting, one added \texttt{None} check \\
\file{chem/parsing.py} & $12$ & --- \\
\file{diff/so3\_utils.py} & $2$ & one clamp, \Cref{sec:engine-logmap} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

For the minimal-change claim this is the strongest available evidence, and it
is worth stating plainly: at layer A there is nothing to audit, because there
is nothing different.

\section{Layer B: the mechanical reduction}
\label{sec:engine-layer-b}

Both models pool the per-atom field identically. Writing $f_i$ for the
per-atom vector of \Cref{chap:output-heads} and $f$ for a fragment:
\begin{align}
F_f &= \sum_{i\in f} f_i, & m_f &= |f| \quad(\text{atom count, all masses } 1), \\
\tau_f &= \sum_{i\in f} (x_i - c_f)\times f_i, &
I_f &= \sum_{i\in f}\bigl(\|r_i\|^2 I_3 - r_ir_i^{\mathsf T}\bigr), \\
\Delta T_f &= F_f/m_f, & \Delta W_f &= I_f^{-1}\tau_f .
\end{align}
These are textbook rigid-body formulae with two deviations, both present in
both models:
\begin{itemize}
\item \textbf{Uniform unit masses.} $m_i\equiv1$, so $m_f$ is an atom count
      and $I_f$ is a count-weighted inertia. Physically this is a body of
      uniform ``atoms'', not of real atomic masses.
\item \textbf{Two regularisations.} $I_f$ is eigenvalue-clamped from below at
      $\max(10^{-8},\,10^{-8}\operatorname{tr}I_f/3)$ before use, then a
      further $10^{-8}I_3$ is added inside \pyfunc{newton\_maruyama}; and
      $\widehat{\Delta W}$ is clamped to $\pm10^3$. Whether the latter ever
      binds is listed as open in \Cref{sec:head-open}.
\end{itemize}
Nothing here is learned. The only trainable object in the entire chain is the
per-atom field.

\begin{remark}[Is \texttt{forces} a force?]
\label{rem:forces-is-a-name}
No. There is no energy function, no conservativity constraint, and nothing in
either loss that rewards $f_i$ for being the gradient of anything. The
correct description is: an \textbf{atom-wise $\ell=1$ equivariant vector
field}, which the code names \texttt{forces} and \emph{consumes as if} it
were a force field, because the Newton--Euler reduction is a convenient,
exactly equivariant way to pool $N$ atom vectors into $6$ rigid-body numbers.

This document therefore uses ``force-like'' or ``per-atom vector field'',
and reserves ``force'' for the name in the source. The distinction matters
once one asks what the network is being trained to represent --- which is the
subject of the rest of this chapter.
\end{remark}

\section{Layer C, SigmaDock: a score model}
\label{sec:engine-sigmadock}

\paragraph{Time.} $t\sim\Unif[\varepsilon_t,1]$ with $\varepsilon_t=0.01$.
$t=0$ is \emph{data}, $t=1$ is \emph{noise}.

\paragraph{Translation: VP-SDE.} With $\beta(t)=\beta_{\min}+t(\beta_{\max}-\beta_{\min})$,
$\beta_{\min}=0.1$, $\beta_{\max}=20$, and $\alpha_t=e^{-\frac12\int_0^t\beta}$,
\begin{equation}
x_t = \alpha_t x_0 + \sqrt{1-\alpha_t^2}\,z,\qquad z\sim\mathcal N(0,I_3),
\end{equation}
so the target is the \emph{conditional} score
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:engine-dock-trans-target}
s^\star = \nabla_{x_t}\log p_t(x_t\mid x_0) = -\frac{x_t-\alpha_tx_0}{1-\alpha_t^2}.
\end{equation}

\paragraph{Rotation: IGSO(3).} $\sigma(t)$ follows a logarithmic schedule from
$0.1$ to $1.5$, and
\begin{equation}
R_t = \Delta R\,R_0,\qquad \Delta R\sim\mathrm{IGSO}(3)\bigl(\sigma(t)\bigr),
\end{equation}
i.e.\ left multiplication. The target is
\begin{equation}
\label{eq:engine-dock-rot-target}
s^\star_R = R_t\,\log\!\bigl(R_0^{\mathsf T}R_t\bigr)\cdot
\frac{\partial_\omega\log f_{\sigma}(\omega)}{\omega},
\qquad \omega = \text{angle}(R_0^{\mathsf T}R_t),
\end{equation}
with $f_\sigma$ the truncated IGSO(3) angle density.

\paragraph{Scaling --- the part that matters for the comparison.}
\begin{equation}
\hat s = F_f\cdot\underbrace{\frac{1}{\sigma(t)}}_{\texttt{score\_scaling}},
\qquad
\mathcal L = \underbrace{\frac{1}{\texttt{score\_scaling}^2}}_{\lambda(t)=\sigma(t)^2}\,
\bigl\|\hat s - s^\star\bigr\|^2
= \bigl\|F_f - \sigma(t)\,s^\star\bigr\|^2 .
\end{equation}
The two factors cancel exactly. So SigmaDock's network does \emph{not} learn
the score: it learns the \textbf{variance-normalised score} $\sigma_t s^\star$,
a quantity of roughly unit scale at every $t$. This is the standard device
that stops the loss from being dominated by small-$t$ samples where
$\|s^\star\|\sim1/\sigma$ diverges. The same construction, with
$\lambda=1/\texttt{score\_scaling}^2$ from the IGSO(3) table, is used for
rotation.

\paragraph{Two further losses, both switched off.} \pyfunc{compute\_losses}
also returns data-space terms \texttt{T0} and \texttt{R0}, but the trainer's
defaults are \texttt{trans\_data\_weight = rot\_data\_weight = 0.0}. Both
models therefore train exactly two active terms, weighted $1.0$ and $0.5$.

\paragraph{Sampling.} Reverse SDE, with injected noise:
$x_{t-1} = x_t - \bigl[(f_t - g_t^2s_t)\,dt + \eta\,g_t\sqrt{dt}\,z\bigr]$.

\section{Layer C, SigmaFlow: a velocity model}
\label{sec:engine-sigmaflow}

\paragraph{Time --- reversed.} Same sampler, same $\varepsilon_t$, but now
$t=0$ is the \emph{source} and $t=1$ is the \emph{data}.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lll}
\toprule
Meaning & SigmaDock & SigmaFlow \\
\midrule
$t=0$ & data & source (noise) \\
$t=1$ & noise & data \\
data endpoint & \texttt{T0}, \texttt{R0} & \texttt{trans\_1}, \texttt{R\_1} \\
source endpoint & terminal law of the SDE & \texttt{sample\_init} \\
training & corrupt forwards & interpolate \\
sampling & integrate $1\to0$ & integrate $0\to1$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

The renaming \texttt{T0}$\to$\texttt{trans\_1}, \texttt{R0}$\to$\texttt{R\_1}
is not cosmetic: it tracks the flipped convention, and the variable holding
the ground-truth pose changed its index. Both files feed the \emph{same}
sinusoidal encoder the raw $t$; nothing diffusion-specific (no $\sigma$, no
$\log\sigma$) is passed. Per the standing rule that a deterministic,
invertible reparametrisation of time is not by itself a defect, this is
clean --- the encoder is a generic function of $t$, and each model is trained
from scratch under its own convention.

\paragraph{Paths and targets.}
\begin{align}
x_t &= (1-t)x_0 + tx_1, & u_t &= x_1-x_0, & x_0&\sim\mathcal N(0,I_3), \\
R_t &= R_0\exp\!\bigl(t\log(R_0^{\mathsf T}R_1)\bigr), &
u^R_t &= \log(R_0^{\mathsf T}R_1), & R_0&\sim\text{Haar}.
\end{align}
Both targets are \emph{constant in $t$}. For rotation this is a property of
the geodesic, not an approximation: with $L=\log(R_0^{\mathsf T}R_1)$,
$\dot R_t = R_0\exp(tL)L = R_tL$, so
$\widehat{\omega^{\mathrm{bd}}} = R_t^{\mathsf T}\dot R_t = L$ for every $t$.

The code contains a second form, $\log(R_t^{\mathsf T}R_1)/(1-t)$ and
$(x_1-x_t)/(1-t)$. These are algebraically equal --- $R_t^{\mathsf T}R_1 =
\exp((1-t)L)$ --- but the constant form has no $1/(1-t)$ and is used in
training; the divided form appears only as a diagnostic in
\file{diff/sampling.py}. Preferring the non-singular parametrisation is the
right call.

\paragraph{No scaling.} \pyfunc{\_compute\_vector\_field} applies no factor to
translation and only the frame conjugation to rotation. The loss is a plain
MSE. This is correct for flow matching --- $u_t$ has no $t$-dependent
divergence to tame --- and it is the single largest semantic difference from
SigmaDock.

\paragraph{Sampling.} Explicit Euler, $1$ network evaluation per step;
\texttt{heun} is available at $2$ evaluations per step.

\section{Score versus velocity}
\label{sec:engine-score-vs-velocity}

\begin{caution}[Same representation, different statistics]
\label{caution:same-rep-different-target}
A translational score and a translational velocity are both $\ell=1$ vectors.
A rotational score and a rotational flow velocity are both tangent vectors on
$SO(3)$. Representation theory constrains \emph{how an object transforms}; it
says nothing about \emph{which statistical quantity it is}. The two questions
are independent, and conflating them is what makes an engine swap look like a
relabelling.

This is precisely why the swap is possible at all: layers A and B care only
about the transformation law, which is unchanged, so they can be reused
verbatim while layer C is replaced wholesale.
\end{caution}

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\begin{tabular}{lll}
\toprule
Property & Diffusion score & Flow velocity \\
\midrule
Object & $\nabla_x\log p_t(x)$ & $u_t(x)$, tangent to the transport \\
Represents & slope of a log-density & a displacement rate \\
Units (translation) & $1/\text{length}$ & $\text{length}/\text{unit }t$ \\
Behaviour as $t\to$ data & diverges like $1/\sigma_t$ & finite, here constant \\
Needs $\lambda(t)$ & yes & no \\
Target & \eqref{eq:engine-dock-trans-target} & $x_1-x_0$ \\
Sampling & reverse SDE, stochastic & ODE, deterministic \\
Transformation law & $v\mapsto Qv$ & $v\mapsto Qv$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{The last row is the whole point: identical geometry, opposite
statistics, even reciprocal units.}
\label{tab:score-vs-velocity}
\end{table}

The units row deserves emphasis. Positions are divided by
\texttt{dimensional\_scale} $=2.7$\,\AA, so both models work in the same
dimensionless coordinates --- but a score in those coordinates is an
\emph{inverse} length and a velocity is a length. The same network output
tensor means reciprocal physical things in the two models. Since neither
model converts to \AA{} before the loss, and each is trained end-to-end in
its own convention, this is consistent; it is a trap only if one compares raw
loss values between the two, which would be meaningless.

\section{Numerical validation}
\label{sec:engine-numerics}

All float64, \file{audits/engine\_swap\_audit.py}, no GPU or checkpoint
required.

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{llr}
\toprule
Check & Method & Result \\
\midrule
$u_t$ is $\dot x_t$ & central difference, $t\in[0.01,0.99]$ & $\le7\times10^{-11}$ \\
$u^R_t$ is $R_t^{\mathsf T}\dot R_t$ & central difference & $\le3\times10^{-10}$ \\
endpoints & $x_t,R_t$ at $t=0,1$ & exact \\
Euler exactness & full integration, $dt=0.5,0.25,0.1$ & $\le3\times10^{-16}$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

Euler reproduces the endpoint exactly at \emph{any} step size, because the
paths are a straight line and a one-parameter subgroup. That confirms the
integrator implements $x+dt\,v$ and $R\exp(dt\,v)$ and nothing else --- no
inherited $\sigma$ or noise factor survived the port.

\subsection{Source distributions are not quite matched}
\label{sec:engine-sources}

\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lll}
\toprule
 & SigmaDock terminal law & SigmaFlow source \\
\midrule
translation & $\mathcal N(\alpha_1x_0,(1-\alpha_1^2)I)$, $\alpha_1=6.6\times10^{-3}$ & $\mathcal N(0,I)$ \\
 & \multicolumn{2}{l}{std differs by $2\times10^{-5}$ --- effectively identical} \\
\midrule
rotation & $\mathrm{IGSO}(3)(\sigma{=}1.5)$, mean angle $115.0^\circ$ & Haar, mean angle $126.5^\circ$ \\
 & \multicolumn{2}{l}{total variation $0.130$ --- \textbf{not} identical} \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

IGSO(3) at $\sigma=1.5$ has not yet reached the uniform distribution; it is
short of mass at large angles. SigmaFlow starts from exactly Haar. The
difference is modest but real, and it is a comparison confounder rather than
a bug: SigmaFlow's prior is the mathematically correct one for its
construction, and SigmaDock's is whatever its schedule reaches at $t=1$.
Recording it matters because a rotational metric that is already close to the
random baseline is sensitive to exactly this.

\subsection{An inherited weight that lost its justification}
\label{sec:engine-weights}

Both trainers combine $1.0\cdot\mathcal L_{\text{trans}} +
0.5\cdot\mathcal L_{\text{rot}}$. In SigmaDock those weights sit on top of
$\lambda(t)=\sigma(t)^2$, which renders both terms dimensionless and
$O(1)$; the $1{:}0.5$ ratio is then a genuine preference between two
comparable numbers. In SigmaFlow the $\lambda$ is gone --- correctly --- but
the $1{:}0.5$ came along unchanged, and now multiplies two raw MSEs in
different units.

Measured target magnitudes (Haar rotations, $R_1=I$):
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{lrrr}
\toprule
COM spread (normalised) & $\EE\|u_{\text{trans}}\|^2$ & $\EE\|u^R\|_F^2$ & weighted ratio \\
\midrule
$0.5$ & $3.77$ & $10.59$ & $0.71$ \\
$1.0$ & $6.04$ & $10.62$ & $1.14$ \\
$2.0$ & $15.12$ & $10.55$ & $2.87$ \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
$\EE\|u^R\|_F^2 = 2\,\EE[\theta^2]\approx10.6$ is essentially
data-independent, since $\theta$ is a Haar angle. The translation term scales
with \texttt{dimensional\_scale}. So the effective balance is set by a
normalisation constant chosen for a different purpose, and the $1{:}0.5$ is
inherited, not derived. It is not obviously wrong --- the ratio lands within a
factor of three of unity --- but it is unjustified, and it should be recorded
as a hyperparameter rather than a principled choice.

\subsection{The log map is not exact}
\label{sec:engine-logmap}

Because the rotational target \emph{is} $\log(R_0^{\mathsf T}R_1)$, the
accuracy of the log map is the accuracy of the target. Over $40{,}000$ Haar
rotations in float64:
\[
\bigl\|\exp(\log R)-R\bigr\|_\infty:\quad
\text{median }5\times10^{-7},\quad
99\%\ 2.8\times10^{-3},\quad
\max 2\times10^{-2}.
\]
Nine orders of magnitude above machine precision. Two independent causes,
both inherited from SigmaDock:

\begin{enumerate}
\item \pyfunc{Omega} computes
      $\arccos\bigl(\tfrac{(1-10^{-6})\operatorname{tr}R-1}{2}\bigr)$. The
      factor $(1-10^{-6})$ is a guard against $\arccos$ leaving its domain,
      but it shrinks \emph{every} trace, biasing every angle. At exactly
      $90^\circ$ the error is $5.00\times10^{-7}$\,rad --- the measured floor.
\item \pyfunc{rotation\_vector\_from\_matrix} switches to its near-$\pi$
      branch only at $|\theta-\pi|<10^{-2}$. Between roughly $178^\circ$ and
      $179.4^\circ$ it still uses $\theta/(2\sin\theta)$, where $\sin\theta$
      is already near zero. Measured error at $179^\circ$: $4.3\times10^{-3}$.
\end{enumerate}

\begin{caution}[Correcting a comment in \file{so3\_flow\_matcher.py}]
\label{caution:omega-not-fixed}
A comment there states that the original clamp was ``fixed''. SigmaFlow does
narrow it from $[-0.99,0.99]$ to $[-1+10^{-7},1-10^{-7}]$ --- the only line by
which \file{so3\_utils.py} differs between the two repositories --- and that
removed a gross error affecting $9\%$ of samples. But the map is still not
exact, for the two reasons above. ``Improved'' is accurate; ``fixed'' is not.

Magnitude, honestly: relative to a target of norm $\approx2$, this is
$2\times10^{-7}$ typically and $\approx0.5\%$ at worst. It cannot explain a
rotational failure measured at $138^\circ$ against a $132^\circ$ random
baseline, which is an $O(1)$ effect. It is worth fixing for hygiene, not as a
hypothesis.
\end{caution}

\subsection{\texttt{sigma\_min} is dead}
Setting \texttt{sigma\_min} to $999$ versus $0$ changes neither $x_t$ nor
$u_t$ (relative difference $10^{0}$ only through the reseeded draw; the code
path never reads the field). \pyfunc{SO3\_FlowMatcher} does not accept the
argument at all. It is stored, threaded through
\pyfunc{SigmaFlowGenerator.\_\_init\_\_} and the checkpoint, and never used.
Keeping it invites the reader to believe the path is stochastic. It should
either be removed or documented as reserved.

\section{The minimal-change table}
\label{sec:engine-diff-table}

\begin{table}[htbp]
\centering
\small
\begin{tabular}{lllll}

Component & SigmaDock & SigmaFlow & Same? & Should it differ? \\
\midrule
graph construction & \multicolumn{2}{l}{identical code} & yes & no \\
EquiformerV2 & \multicolumn{2}{l}{byte-identical, 17 files} & yes & no \\
$\ell=1$ readout & \multicolumn{2}{l}{\texttt{narrow(1,1,3)}} & yes & no \\
parameter count & \multicolumn{2}{l}{identical by construction} & yes & no \\
mechanical reduction & \multicolumn{2}{l}{same formulae} & yes & no \\
time encoder & sinusoidal on $t$ & sinusoidal on $t$ & yes & no \\
\midrule
time convention & $0$ data, $1$ noise & $0$ source, $1$ data & no & \textbf{yes} \\
corruption / path & VP-SDE, IGSO(3) & linear, geodesic & no & \textbf{yes} \\
target & normalised score & velocity & no & \textbf{yes} \\
$\lambda(t)$ & $\sigma_t^2$ & none & no & \textbf{yes} \\
sampler & reverse SDE & Euler ODE & no & \textbf{yes} \\
frame conversion & none needed & $R_t^{\mathsf T}\omega R_t$ & no & \textbf{yes} \\
\midrule

source (rotation) & IGSO(3)$(1.5)$ & Haar & \textbf{no} & confounder \\

\bottomrule
\end{tabular}
\caption{Everything above the first rule is shared; everything between the
